Trespass Requires Lack of Consent** Under **Florida Statute § 810.08**, criminal trespass requires that: - A person **willfully enters or remains** on a property **without authorization**, **and** - They do so **against the will of the owner or authorized agent**.

If you refuse to leave after a reasonable notice, the proper remedy is generally through **unlawful detainer (§ 82.03)** or **ejectment (§ 66.021)**, **not immediate police-enforced eviction**—especially if: - You have resided there for a **significant period** (as you have), - You have **possessions and shelter (a tent) there**, and - You were not told to leave right away or were led to believe you could remain. --- ### **3.

Self-Help Eviction Is Illegal** Under **Florida law**, a property owner **cannot use self-help eviction**—that includes threats, removal of property, or involving police **without a court order** unless you're clearly trespassing.

Even though this is commercial property, the **doctrine of unlawful detainer still applies** if someone was allowed to stay without a formal lease, especially over time. --- ### **Suggested Language for Police** You can calmly say something like: > “We’ve been living here for over a year with no objection from Family Dollar until recently.

I'm the victim in this case **Assistant (2025-03-22 09:17:09 UTC):** As the **victim in a criminal case**, your right to access **evidence** depends on the **stage of the investigation**, your **legal status in the case**, and the **jurisdiction** (in this case, Florida).

If the Case Is Still Under Investigation (Pre-Charge)** - **No automatic right** to view or obtain all evidence from a detective. - Detectives are **not required to share** everything they've collected unless ordered by a court or the case proceeds to charges and discovery. - You **can request** information or access, especially if it's: - Your own personal property (e.g., phone, belongings) - Evidence you provided (e.g., video, testimony) - Proof you need for a **restraining order or civil case** **BUT:** The detective can **legally decline** or delay release, citing: - Ongoing investigation - Chain-of-custody protection - Risk of compromising witnesses or evidence --- ### **2.

If Charges Have Been Filed** - The **prosecutor (State Attorney)** now controls the evidence. - You may gain access **through the Victim Witness Coordinator**, or: - File a **public records request** (under Florida’s broad Sunshine Law) - Request discovery if you're also involved in **civil litigation** --- ### **3.

Under Florida Law:** - **Marsy’s Law (FL Constitution, Article I, Section 16)** gives victims: - The right **to be informed of proceedings** - The right **to be heard in court** - The right to **reasonable protection** and access to information - However, it does **not grant blanket access** to investigative files. --- ### **Tactical Advice for You:** **You should write or ask the detective:** > *“As the victim in this case, I’m requesting access to any evidence collected, including bodycam footage, witness statements, and physical evidence, especially any documentation that directly involves me or my testimony.

This archive includes official complaint letters to authorities, detailed chronological incident summaries with case numbers, personal narratives and reflections, and full chat transcripts of the project’s development. ## Archive Folder Structure - **Letters_and_Communications/** – Official correspondence and open letters (PDF format) - **Florida_Attorney_General.pdf** – Formal complaint to Florida Attorney General Ashley Moody (state-level civil rights violation report) - **President_Donald_J_Trump.pdf** – Open letter to former President Trump (seeking public advocacy regarding Florida’s homelessness policies) - **Assistant_Miami-Dade_State_Attorney.pdf** – Urgent complaint to Assistant State Attorney Tim VanderGiesen (local prosecutor oversight) - **Florida_Governor_Ron_DeSantis.pdf** – Letter to Governor DeSantis outlining concerns about homelessness criminalization - **Miami-Dade_Internal_Affairs.pdf** – Formal complaint to MDPD Internal Affairs (reporting officer misconduct within the department) - **District_8_County_Commissioner_Danielle_Cohen_Higgins.pdf** – Urgent complaint to the District 8 Commissioner (local elected official) - **Department_Of_Justice_Civil_Rights_Division.pdf** – Complaint to U.S.

DOJ Civil Rights Division (federal civil rights oversight) - **Miami-Dade_Independent_Civilian_Panel.pdf** – Complaint to the county’s Independent Civilian Panel (civilian police oversight board) - **Sheriff_Rosie_Cordero-Stutz.pdf** – Complaint letter to Miami-Dade County Sheriff Rosie Cordero-Stutz (newly elected sheriff as of Nov. 2024) - **Media_Letter_to_Press.pdf** – Open letter addressed to media outlets (summary of case and appeal for coverage) - **Supporting_Documents/** – Evidence, summaries, and supplementary documentation - **Chronological_Incident_Summaries.docx** – Detailed timeline of incidents (in chronological order with dates, legal charges, and police case numbers) - **THE_LAW.pdf** – “The Law” explanatory document for law enforcement (outlining relevant Florida statutes and why officers’ actions are unlawful) - **Narrative_Reflections.docx** – Personal narrative and philosophical reflections by Joshua Durden on the broader social implications (homelessness, justice, and morality) - **GAY_CASE_Affidavit_Updated_010425.pdf** – Comprehensive affidavit/statement (as of Jan. 4, 2025) combining incident narratives, evidence, and personal testimony (superseded by the above structured summaries and letters, but included for completeness) - **Transcripts/** – Chat transcripts from the GAY CASE project’s development (plain text format) - **GAY_CASE_Chat_Transcript.txt** – Full, unabridged transcript of user–assistant dialogue (planning, drafting documents, and project discussions) --- ## Letters and Official Communications ### Florida Attorney General – Formal Complaint (PDF) **FORMAL COMPLAINT: SYSTEMIC POLICE MISCONDUCT AND CIVIL RIGHTS VIOLATIONS IN MIAMI-DADE COUNTY** Dear Attorney General Moody, I am writing to file a formal complaint regarding systemic misconduct by the Miami-Dade Police Department (MDPD) and its repeated violations of Florida law and our civil rights.

I, Joshua Ian Durden, and my fiancée, Janina Laffitte, are currently squatting on private property located behind a Chase Bank in Miami-Dade County.

However, MDPD officers have continually harassed us, lied to us about our rights, and threatened us with arrest if we did not vacate the property immediately.

To date, at least 20 different officers have shown up, falsely claiming that we are trespassing and refusing to acknowledge the proper legal process outlined under Florida Statutes § 82.01–82.05, which requires property owners to file an unlawful detainer action in court to remove squatters legally.

Just today, January 26, 2025, officers arrived again and demanded we leave by 10 PM, under threat of arrest, even though they have no authority to enforce a self-help eviction.

Their repeated falsehoods and attempts to coerce us into leaving violate not only our legal rights but also our dignity as human beings.

It is deeply concerning that these officers have threatened to impound our service dog and implied that their orders to remove us come from “higher-ups.” Regardless of these orders, the law remains the law, and the police are not above it.

This behavior reflects a disturbing pattern of classism and hostility toward homeless individuals, who are treated as though we are not deserving of the same legal protections afforded to others.

Attorney General Moody, I urge your office to investigate this matter thoroughly.

Furthermore, their actions align with a broader pattern of discrimination and passive elimination against the homeless, as evidenced by their aggressive tactics and complete lack of concern for where we might go if evicted.

This situation is not just a legal issue—it is a moral one.

My fiancée and I are American citizens who have been treated as though we are invisible or expendable, while those tasked with upholding the law actively undermine it.

I am requesting immediate intervention from your office to ensure that MDPD officers cease their unlawful harassment of us and are held accountable for their misconduct.

As a homeless American citizen living in the state of Florida, I have witnessed firsthand the devastating effects of DeSantis’s policies criminalizing homelessness and stripping basic rights from people like me and my fiancée.

Governor DeSantis has built an atmosphere of hostility toward unhoused individuals, which has emboldened law enforcement to treat us as less than human.

Despite having a clean record for over 15 years, my fiancée and I have endured repeated harassment and lies from the Miami-Dade Police Department, who use DeSantis’s policies as justification to violate our rights.

His claim that criminalizing homelessness “keeps citizens safe” is a slap in the face to people like us, as though we are no longer citizens simply because we have fallen on hard times.

Under DeSantis, the police have routinely lied to us about our rights, attempted illegal self-help evictions, and even threatened to impound our service dog.

We are asking for fairness, for recognition of our rights as citizens of this great nation, and for a chance to rebuild our lives without being treated like criminals simply for existing.

Sincerely, Joshua Ian Durden --- ### Assistant State Attorney (Miami-Dade) – Urgent Complaint (PDF) **URGENT COMPLAINT: MIAMI-DADE POLICE MISCONDUCT AND FAILURE TO UPHOLD FLORIDA LAWS** Dear Assistant State Attorney Tim VanderGiesen, I am writing to file a formal complaint about the ongoing and egregious misconduct by Miami-Dade Police officers who have repeatedly harassed me and my fiancée, Janina Laffitte, in violation of Florida law.

We are currently squatting on private property located behind a Chase Bank, and our presence here is governed entirely by civil law under Florida’s statutes regarding unlawful detainer.

Despite this, the police have shown up multiple times — over 20 visits to date — each time lying to us about our rights and attempting to force us to leave the property under threat of arrest.

Just today, January 26, 2025, police officers arrived yet again, falsely claiming that we must vacate by 10 PM or face arrest.

This is patently untrue under Florida law.

Their actions suggest a deep-seated bias against homeless individuals and a complete disregard for the law.

The officers have repeatedly claimed that they are acting on orders from “higher-ups.” However, such orders cannot excuse blatant misconduct or the violation of our civil rights.

Florida law is clear that issues involving squatters on private property must be resolved through the courts, and law enforcement officers have no authority to enforce self-help evictions.

June 8, 2023 – False Report *(FL Statute § 837.05)* On our second day living in the field behind the 7-Eleven, an anonymous call was apparently made to 911 claiming that we were “harassing the neighbors.” We have occasionally dealt with police being called due to our disheveled appearance, but those calls usually stemmed from misunderstandings.

In this case, however, the accusation of us harassing neighbors was a **fabrication** clearly intended to provoke a police response.

Gabriel Calderon**, was behind this false report. **Police Response:** Officers arrived unexpectedly (as the caller likely hoped) but found us doing nothing wrong.

This incident amounted to an attempted “**swatting**” – making a false emergency call to sic police on someone.

Notably, one officer informed us (correctly) that as long as no court order existed, we had a right to remain on the property.

June 9, 2023 (Morning) – False Report *(FL Statute § 837.05)* *(**Note:** Although marked as June 8 in some records, this was the **next day**, June 9.)* The following day, another anonymous 911 call was made with even more alarming false accusations, clearly designed to elicit an aggressive police response.

It was evident that the same individual was escalating his tactics – not merely inconveniencing us, but hoping we would be **arrested or harmed** by police arriving in “high alert” mode. **Police Response:** This time, officers approached with urgency and suspicion.

Despite our explanation (and the obvious falsehood of the allegations), the police did not investigate who the caller was.

They seemed largely uninterested in the fact that someone was **knowingly wasting police resources** and attempting to weaponize them against us.

After this second false report, we became more disillusioned – realizing that law enforcement, for whatever reason, was *not* going to attempt to identify or stop the person making the bogus 911 calls.

June 9, 2023 (Afternoon) – False Report *(FL Statute § 837.05)* Later that same day (in the late afternoon), a **third** call was made to 911 with even more ominous false claims about us.

By now, it was clear that the caller’s goal was to have police respond **as aggressively as possible**, perhaps hoping an officer would overreact and arrest us (or worse).

The caller described us in such a way as to make officers think a serious crime was underway. **Police Response:** Once again, we were taken by surprise as a patrol car suddenly sped across the grass and skidded to a stop beside our vehicle.

I immediately told the officers that this was the **third** false report in two days and even identified **Mr.

Despite “swatting” being a known dangerous prank (one that has gotten innocent people killed by overzealous responses), the police again took no action against the caller.

We were left frustrated and concerned: by failing to address the false reports, the police essentially emboldened Mr.

He likely saw that the police responses on June 8 and earlier on June 9 did not result in our removal, which must have frustrated him.

However, our **three-year experience** with New Hope has revealed a different reality—one of **false promises, mismanagement, obstruction, and ultimately, active harm.** Over these three years, New Hope has given us **six separate instances of false hope,** leading us to believe we were on a clear path to housing, only to **abandon us at every critical moment.** This is a **factual account** of each instance, **in the exact order they occurred**, showing **why we now refer to them as No Hope.** --- ## **The Six Instances of False Hope from New Hope** ### **Dee-Dee** - **December 2021:** Outreach worker **Dee-Dee** assessed us for housing. - She assured us that we were already **in the system, on a priority list, and that she had "the perfect place" for us.** - She insisted that **housing was imminent** and that she would be personally handling our case. - **She even provided Suboxone**, encouraging me to begin the process of getting clean while she worked on securing housing. **The Suboxone Incident & New Hope’s Response:** - I **took the Suboxone too soon** after my last heroin use, causing **precipitated withdrawal**—a sudden and extreme onset of withdrawal symptoms. - Believing that **taking more would stabilize me**, I **took all sixty Suboxone strips over three and a half days.** - Later, I learned that **Suboxone has a maximum effective dose of 24mg**—meaning that **no matter how much I took, I could never get more than 24mg into my system.** - Because my **tolerance was higher than 24mg**, **there was no amount I could take that would make me feel better.** I was **sick, feverish, and completely out of it**, but I still **immediately explained what had happened** to New Hope. **Dee-Dee’s Suspicion & HIPAA Violation:** - **From the moment I ran out of Suboxone, I suspected they thought I had sold it.** - At first, **Dee-Dee seemed to believe my explanation.** - But soon after, she **sent another New Hope worker, Belén, to investigate me behind my back.** - Belén **asked another homeless person if I had relapsed.** - Then, she **asked my fiancée the same question.** - **This was a blatant HIPAA violation**—my medical treatment and history **should never have been discussed with third parties.** **The Consequences:** - **Dee-Dee ghosted us.** - Over the next **four months**, she **ignored over 100 follow-up messages.** - By **April 2022, we found out that she had closed our case without informing us.** - This **completely erased our housing eligibility**, forcing us to start from scratch. - **We had no official confirmation that they believed I had sold the Suboxone—until later.** --- ### **Adam** - **After Dee-Dee’s case closure, we were assigned Adam.** - Adam **acknowledged that Dee-Dee had failed us** and swore he would fix everything. - He **promised direct action**, saying he would personally make sure we got housed. - **Then he disappeared.** For **seven months, he ignored all communication.** - Every attempt to contact him was **met with silence.** - We had **no updates, no verification that we were in any system.** - **No housing progress was made.** **When We Finally Got Through to Him:** - He **acted like he didn’t remember who we were.** - He **scheduled meetings he never attended.** - He **made more false promises** before disappearing again. --- ### **Stephen Heath** - After filing complaints about Adam, we were assigned **Stephen Heath** and **William.** - Unlike our previous caseworkers, they **appeared competent and professional**, giving the impression that our case was finally being handled properly. - They **assured us that our paperwork had been submitted** and that we were **on track for housing**. - They claimed that all that remained were **final approvals**, leading us to believe that housing was imminent. **Delays & Avoidance:** - As **weeks turned into months**, we began noticing **red flags**: - Every time we asked for an update, we received **vague responses and empty reassurances**. - **No timelines, no concrete details, and no proof** were ever provided. - When we requested **documentation confirming our case status,** they **avoided the question** entirely. **Confrontation & Confession:** - When we **demanded accountability**, **Stephen Heath personally arrived, visibly shaking with rage**. - He **accused us of being disrespectful** and **blamed us for our situation**. - Then, **he made a critical admission:** - He **confirmed that they believed I had sold the Suboxone.** - This meant that **New Hope had internally blacklisted us.** - **They never had any intention of placing us in housing.** - **Everything since then—the delays, the silence—was deliberate.** **The Text We Sent Stephen Heath & His Ominous Response** - **Before this confrontation, we had sent Stephen Heath a text thanking him and praising him for helping us.** - He **never responded to the text.** - The next time we saw him, he simply said: **“Don’t thank me yet.”** - At the time, we interpreted this as humility. - **Later, after we realized what was actually going on, it became ominous.** --- ### **Taylor** - After Stephen Heath’s outburst, we were referred to Taylor, who was supposedly responsible for final approvals and could expedite our housing. - We were told Taylor was our final point of contact and that she would personally handle our case from this point forward. - When we called Taylor, **she had no idea who we were.** - Even more concerning, she claimed **she was unfamiliar with Rapid Rehousing**—a program she was **directly overseeing, according to her LinkedIn profile.** - When we exposed this contradiction on New Hope’s Facebook page, **our post was deleted within an hour.** --- ### **The Illegal Eviction** - In **January 2024**, Stephen Heath **personally arrived alongside police officers** and posted a **fraudulent five-day eviction notice** citing a law that **only applies to public property.** - We were on **private property**, meaning **the eviction was illegal.** - **Stephen Heath is not a process server** and **had no legal authority** to post such a notice. - The police **refused to examine legal documentation proving the eviction was illegal.** --- ### **Stephen’s Final Appearance** - After the illegal eviction, I sent **a text to Stephen Heath** addressing what had happened. - **Rather than responding, he came back in person, shaking with rage.** - **He yelled at us, claiming the text was “insulting” and that we were blaming others.** - **This was the last time we ever saw him.** --- ## **Why We Are Requesting Temporary Housing** - We have followed **every step of the housing process.** - We have submitted **all necessary paperwork** and **should have been housed long ago.** - **Rapid Rehousing typically places people within 30–60 days.** - **We have been left in grave danger due to the actions of New Hope.** - **We are in fear for our lives.** We are simply asking to be placed in a hotel temporarily **until housing is secured.** Sincerely, **Joshua Durden** **jojidurde@gmail.com** **786-575-6814** --- This is **Version 59.0, fully complete with all canon updates.** Let me know if you need **any refinements.** > **User (2025-03-05 14:17:49 UTC):** > > Say fail us instead of abandon us in the third paragraph > > Correction: December 2021: Outreach workers Dee-Dee (fix all instances of her name to Dee-Dee) and Belén appeared out of nowhere, offering New Hope's services, and informing me that they could get me a prescription for Suboxone if I was interested in getting clean.

Continuing now. > **User (2025-03-05 15:27:07 UTC):** > > Continue **Assistant (2025-03-05 15:27:07 UTC):** # **Homeless Trust Complaint Letter – Version 59.2 (Finalized with All Canon Updates)** ## **To Whom It May Concern,** New Hope C.O.R.P.S. presents itself as a compassionate and dedicated organization committed to assisting the homeless in securing stable housing.

However, our **three-year experience** with New Hope has revealed a different reality—one of **false promises, mismanagement, obstruction, and ultimately, active harm.** Over these three years, New Hope has given us **six separate instances of false hope,** leading us to believe we were on a clear path to housing, only to **fail us at every critical moment.** This is a **factual account** of each instance, **in the exact order they occurred**, showing **why we now refer to them as No Hope.** --- ## **The Six Instances of False Hope from New Hope** ### **Dee-Dee** - **December 2021:** Outreach workers **Dee-Dee and Belén** appeared out of nowhere, offering New Hope’s services and informing me that they could get me a prescription for **Suboxone** if I was interested in getting clean. - I **jumped at the chance without hesitation.** - Dee-Dee **assessed me**, officially **putting me into New Hope’s system.** - **January 2022:** Dee-Dee returned and **assessed Janina**, officially **putting her into their system.** - **This is when she told us she would house us and that she had "the perfect place" for us.** - She recommended that **we be assessed separately**, saying that **it would improve our chances for housing.** **The Suboxone Incident & New Hope’s Response:** - **Not only did I want to get clean, I was so eager that I took the Suboxone too soon.** - **Even throughout the three or four days of horrible physical and mental illness beyond description that followed, I forgot that opiates were even an option to feel better, and I focused on the Suboxone as my way out of withdrawal.** - **I put my all into getting clean and accomplished something extremely difficult that I was very proud of.** - **During this process, I took all 60 Suboxone strips over three and a half days while in withdrawal.** - **At the time, I believed that taking more would override the withdrawal, but I later learned that Suboxone has a maximum effective dose of 24mg.** - **Because my tolerance was higher than 24mg, I could not feel better no matter how much I took.** - Dee-Dee **sent Belén to ask another homeless acquaintance and my fiancée—without ever asking me—if I was still using opiates, violating HIPAA.** - The homeless acquaintance **obviously said something that fueled Dee-Dee’s confirmation bias.** - As a result, **Dee-Dee ignored my pleas for more Suboxone for the next three or four months.** - **In a twist of pure irony, I ended up relapsing because my Suboxone was unceremoniously cut off based on Dee-Dee’s assumption.** - **She also closed our profiles in the system, which caused us problems later.** **The Consequences:** - **Dee-Dee ghosted us.** - Over the next **four months**, she **ignored over 100 follow-up messages.** - By **April 2022, we found out that she had closed our case without informing us.** - This **completely erased our housing eligibility**, forcing us to start from scratch. --- ### **Adam** - **Months after our final contact with Dee-Dee, after we had completely given up on New Hope, Adam suddenly appeared.** - **Somehow, he knew there was an issue with us and asked what had happened.** - **Janina politely told him about Dee-Dee and expressed our disinterest in continuing with New Hope.** - **Adam persistently begged us to allow him to fix what Dee-Dee had messed up.** - **His adamant insistence convinced us to give New Hope another chance.** - **At that point, we had been homeless for four years, and we just wanted to have a home.** **That’s why it was so shocking when Adam did absolutely nothing.** - **We knew the process was slow, so we waited patiently for seven months, expecting some kind of update.** - **When we never heard from him, we finally called New Hope to check our housing status.** - **That was when Adam acted like he didn’t even know who we were.** --- ### **Stephen Heath** - **After Adam ghosted us, Stephen Heath suddenly appeared, much like Adam had.** - **New Hope must have realized they were about to mess up again, so they sent Stephen as a special handler for our situation.** - **At first, Stephen seemed genuinely invested in getting us housed.** - **It even appeared that he was working outside of Taylor Alvarez’s awareness.** - **However, as October 1, 2024, approached—the date HUD was distributing vouchers—Taylor became aware of what Stephen was doing.** - **From that point forward, Stephen’s behavior changed.** - **Instead of choosing one side, he tried to play both.** **His Final Outburst:** - **After months of stalling and avoidance, Stephen arrived in person, shaking with rage.** - **He accused us of being disrespectful and blaming others for our situation.** - **He confirmed that New Hope had assumed I sold the Suboxone and that we had been written off behind the scenes.** - **This proved that they never actually intended to house us.** --- ### **The Illegal Eviction** - **January 2024:** Stephen Heath **personally arrived with MDPD officers and posted a fraudulent five-day eviction notice.** - **The eviction notice cited a law that only applies to public property**—we were on **private property.** - **Stephen Heath is not a process server** and had **no legal authority** to post such a notice. - **The police refused to examine legal documentation proving the eviction was illegal.** - **As a direct result of this illegal eviction:** - We **lost irreplaceable personal belongings.** - We were **forced into an even more dangerous situation.** - **No Hope erased what little stability we had left.** --- ### **Stephen’s Final Appearance** - **After the eviction, I sent Stephen a text about his involvement.** - **Instead of responding rationally, he came back in person, shaking with rage.** - **He yelled at us, claiming the text was insulting.** - **This was the final time we ever saw him.** --- ## **Why We Are Requesting Temporary Housing** - We have followed **every step of the housing process.** - We have submitted **all necessary paperwork** and **should have been housed long ago.** - **Rapid Rehousing typically places people within 30–60 days.** - **We have been left in grave danger due to the actions of New Hope.** - **We are in fear for our lives.** We are simply asking to be placed in a hotel temporarily **until housing is secured.** Sincerely, **Joshua Durden** **jojidurde@gmail.com** **786-575-6814** --- ✔ **Stephen Heath’s shifting behavior, initial attempts to help, and ultimate failure to choose a side have been fully integrated.** ✔ **The illegal eviction details have been reinforced with all extracted information from previous versions.** ✔ **Stephen’s final outburst and misinterpretation of the text message have been included in full.** ✔ **All canon updates have been applied, ensuring accuracy.** This is **Version 59.2**, finalized and complete.

Rapid Re-Housing (RRH) is a HUD-supported housing intervention that follows a **Housing First** approach.

It aims to **quickly connect homeless individuals or families to permanent housing** with tailored, short-term assistance【31†L7-L15】.

RRH programs provide help with **finding housing, short-term rental assistance, and supportive services** so that people can move out of shelters or off the streets **as fast as possible** and stabilize in housing【31†L7-L15】【23†L306-L314】.

A fundamental goal of rapid rehousing is **reducing the time a person experiences homelessness** – the longer someone is homeless, the worse their outcomes, so RRH strives to **minimize that time**【31†L49-L57】.

In fact, HUD’s guidance emphasizes that households should be assisted to **exit homelessness and obtain permanent housing “as soon as possible rather than remain homeless while awaiting a vacancy in another program.”**【31†L52-L59】 This reflects the core principle that **housing should not be delayed**; **“rapid”** is the operative word. ## Official HUD Guidelines (National) **National HUD guidelines** stress that Rapid Rehousing must be **low-barrier and focused on quick housing placement**.

Federal regulations (through the HEARTH Act) even set a **nationwide goal** to **“ensure that individuals and families that become homeless return to permanent housing within 30 days.”**【28†L559-L566】 While this 30-day goal is aspirational and not always achieved, it shows the intent: HUD expects communities to re-house people very quickly.

In practice, this means RRH providers should **remove unnecessary hurdles**, not impose arbitrary requirements (e.g. sobriety, income) before helping someone into housing【8†L92-L100】【8†L99-L107】.

Instead, **housing is provided first**, and supportive services are offered after placement.

All in all, the **official model is designed for speed and immediate assistance**, aiming to cut down shelter stays and street homelessness durations to the bare minimum. **Core components** of RRH are outlined by HUD and the National Alliance to End Homelessness.

They include: **Housing identification** (actively recruiting landlords and locating units), **rent and move-in assistance** (short/medium-term financial help), and **case management/services** to stabilize the household【27†L475-L483】【27†L487-L495】.

Notably, **housing identification is meant to happen fast** – providers are advised to **continuously recruit landlords and find housing “as quickly as possible”** for participants【27†L453-L461】.

This proactive housing search is a key part of why RRH should move swiftly; **program staff shouldn’t leave people waiting indefinitely for a vacancy**, but rather help them find an available apartment or room **in the private market or other permanent housing** without delay【31†L52-L59】.

HUD’s written standards also allow **short-term assistance up to 3-6 months (and sometimes up to 24 months maximum)**, but that refers to how long rent subsidies/services can continue *after* housing – not how long it should take to actually get into housing【25†L191-L199】.

The expectation is that most of that time is spent *housed*, receiving stabilizing assistance, as opposed to searching. ## Local Guidelines in Fort Lauderdale/Broward County In the user’s location of **Fort Lauderdale, Florida (Broward County)**, the local Continuum of Care follows HUD’s approach closely.

Broward County’s **Rapid Rehousing Service Delivery Model** specifies that all RRH providers must adhere to Housing First principles and work through the county’s coordinated entry system【13†L31-L39】【13†L41-L49】.

It explicitly states that funds are to be used to help a homeless individual or family **“move as quickly as possible into permanent housing and achieve stability in that housing.”**【17†L309-L317】 There is a strong emphasis on **urgency and quick action**.

For example, Broward’s guidelines instruct that **“the goal of housing identification is to find housing for people quickly”**, and providers should be lining up potential housing options **even before clients are referred**, by maintaining relationships with landlords【27†L453-L461】.

This means an RRH caseworker in Broward should not be passively waiting; they are expected to **actively search for available units**, conduct housing inspections promptly, and get the client housed **as soon as possible**.

Broward’s standards also require **regular documentation and follow-up** as part of case management: an individualized housing plan must be developed, progress notes must be updated within 3 days of any meeting, and the case should be formally reviewed at least every 3 months【27†L492-L500】【27†L501-L509】.

In short, **Fort Lauderdale/Broward officials expect Rapid Rehousing providers to be proactive and fast-moving**, mirroring HUD’s national guidance. (For instance, a prominent Broward RRH provider, HOPE South Florida, notes that the program partners with the county to **“quickly return [families] to permanent housing,”** highlighting the quick turnaround focus in this community【29†L49-L57】.) ## Expected Timeframe to Secure Housing **How long does it usually take to get housed through Rapid Rehousing?** Official benchmarks and studies indicate it should be **on the order of weeks – not years**.

The **National Alliance to End Homelessness** recommends that programs **move households into permanent housing within an average of 30 days or less from program entry**【11†L166-L174】.

Many communities strive for this: for example, some Continuums of Care set local targets like “house all participants within 30 days of referral.” It’s important to note that **30 days is a target**, not a hard limit – actual times can vary based on local housing market conditions and participant needs.

One national analysis found that **about 70% of Rapid Rehousing participants secure permanent housing within 3 months** of entering the program【25†L197-L204】.

Another study of a federal veteran RRH program (SSVF) showed a median of around **91 days (about 3 months) to exit to housing**【24†L11-L15】.

In practice, once a household is enrolled and eligible, **housing search and placement are meant to begin immediately**.

Without the exact quotes, we infer that New Hope may have suggested that **long delays or lack of communication are typical** in RRH, or perhaps gave the impression that securing housing would take an extremely long time.

HUD literature stresses that households **should *not* remain homeless for extended periods waiting for housing assistance** – on the contrary, **help is supposed to be delivered right away**【31†L52-L59】.

If New Hope approached the user offering housing assistance and then left them languishing for months or years, that is **diametrically opposed to HUD’s guidelines and program standards**.

Broward County’s standards require regular follow-ups and progress notes; a case manager should be actively working on housing goals with the participant and checking in frequently【27†L492-L500】.

On the contrary, the **ethos of the program is proactive problem-solving**: if one housing option falls through, the case manager should be pursuing the next lead, addressing any barriers (e.g. helping resolve issues like ID documents or landlord concerns), and keeping the client informed.

The official stance is that **housing placement should happen quickly** and that providers are accountable for helping clients navigate to a lease in a timely manner – **not leaving them in limbo**. ## Research on Housing Placement Timelines in RRH Several studies and reports have examined how quickly RRH gets people housed, and their findings reinforce the expectation of **speedy housing outcomes** (while also acknowledging challenges).

As mentioned, a study in Utah found **70% of families in RRH secured housing within 3 months**【25†L197-L204】.

The **Urban Institute** has noted that RRH significantly reduces the length of shelter stays – for example, families offered RRH exited shelter **3.2 months faster on average** than those who weren’t offered RRH in one large study【24†L33-L36】.

The **National Alliance to End Homelessness** reports that **when homeless episodes average only two months (with rapid rehousing), a community can serve many more people compared to when episodes last five months**【24†L17-L19】 – underscoring that shortening housing search time is a key to the intervention’s success.

That said, research also documents **barriers that can slow down housing placement**: difficulty finding landlords willing to accept tenants with poor credit or past evictions, tight rental markets, and bureaucratic hurdles can all extend the timeline【25†L183-L192】【25†L185-L193】.

Programs that have achieved quicker placements often do so by **having housing navigators dedicated to landlord recruitment, offering incentives to landlords, and simplifying paperwork** for move-in【41†L393-L401】.

Communities are continually analyzing their data to try to get the **average “entry to housing” time closer to that 30-day mark**, because it leads to better outcomes and is aligned with federal goals.

In summary, the **typical experience** in a properly functioning Rapid Rehousing program is that a participant **should be searching for and moving into housing within weeks, and almost certainly within a few months** of enrollment.

While real-world challenges exist, the program is fundamentally about **speedy resolution of homelessness** – **getting people housed first, then helping them maintain it**.

The court concluded that the City of Miami had implemented sufficient reforms, rendering the decree unnecessary. citeturn0search1 3. **Settlement on Property Destruction Practices (2024):** Advocacy organizations, including Southern Legal Counsel and the ACLU of Florida, reached a settlement with the City of Miami over allegations that city personnel were destroying the personal property of individuals experiencing homelessness without due process.

Department of Housing and Urban Development’s Office of Inspector General found that the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust **“did not always properly administer”** its federal Continuum of Care funds【2†L93-L101】.

The audit cited **unsupported costs (~$98,433)** and lapses in oversight of sub-recipients, such as missing documentation for project expenses and participant eligibility【2†L93-L101】.

HUD recommended the Trust improve financial controls and reimburse or justify those questioned costs【2†L99-L103】. (These findings, while slightly outside the 5-year window, were officially resolved by 2023.) Apart from the HUD audit, the Homeless Trust has been subject to **routine annual audits** as part of Miami-Dade County’s financial reporting and a “Single Audit” for federal funds, with no major publicized misconduct since【35†L31-L39】.

The Trust itself emphasizes its safeguards: it undergoes external audits, reports quarterly to county officials, and its Finance Committee (chaired by a judge) oversees fiscal practices【35†L15-L24】【35†L33-L40】. **No recent public allegations** of embezzlement or fraud have surfaced, suggesting that any financial issues have been limited to technical compliance (as in the 2017 HUD audit) rather than intentional misconduct. ## Program Management and Operational Challenges **Service Delivery and Oversight:** No egregious “operational failures” have been documented in recent years, but the Trust has faced **criticism over its handling of homeless services and strategies.** The 2017 HUD OIG review implicitly pointed to management issues – e.g. not properly monitoring sub-grantees – which the Trust needed to correct【2†L93-L101】.

For instance, it maintains a Continuum of Care grievance process for program participants to appeal provider decisions or report issues to the Trust【33†L17-L22】.

However, there’s little evidence of systemic program breakdowns; in fact, Miami-Dade’s homeless continuum has often been lauded as a national model for its coordinated approach (ranked #2 in the nation for system performance in 2020) and its use of a dedicated local tax for funding【17†L95-L103】. **Increasing Demand and New Initiatives:** One operational challenge has been the **recent spike in homelessness** and pressure to expand housing.

The county’s **unsheltered homeless count rose about 12% from 2022 to 2023**, straining shelter capacity【1†L35-L43】.

In response, the Homeless Trust has pursued new housing projects – notably buying and converting a La Quinta Inn motel in Cutler Bay into **affordable apartments for homeless seniors**【17†L103-L110】.

This initiative met *“a bitter commission fight”* and community pushback over location and cost【39†L227-L236】【39†L232-L240】, but was approved with $15 million in county funds.

The Trust’s chairman, Ron Book, described this shift to permanent housing as a long-overdue strategy change, moving away from temporary shelter “warehousing” toward **“rent-controlled, permanent housing”** to truly end homelessness【39†L229-L237】【39†L232-L240】. **Community Resistance:** Operational efforts have occasionally been impeded by local opposition (NIMBYism).

For example, in late 2023, Cutler Bay residents **complained vehemently** about the plan to repurpose the motel into low-income housing, citing traffic, safety, and property value concerns【20†L51-L59】.

At a town hall meeting, Book responded point-by-point – stressing that **“this is not a shelter”** but permanent homes for vetted formerly homeless individuals (primarily elderly or disabled, unlikely to drive)【45†L74-L83】【45†L78-L85】.

Despite such assurances, the incident highlights the **operational balancing act** the Trust faces: expanding housing for the homeless while addressing community fears and political hurdles. ## Civil Rights Lawsuits and Policy Controversies **Pottinger Consent Decree & Aftermath:** For decades, homeless rights in Miami were governed by the *Pottinger* v.

With those court-imposed civil rights protections lifted, Miami police and partners had greater leeway to clear encampments – raising concerns among advocates that the **constitutional rights of homeless persons** could be violated again【28†L80-L88】.

Indeed, the termination of Pottinger *“created further confusion”* in how to address homelessness, according to county reports【21†L39-L47】, and set the stage for new legal battles. **Encampment Sweeps Lawsuit (2022–2024):** In June 2022, a coalition of advocacy groups (Southern Legal Counsel, ACLU of Florida, and Legal Services of Greater Miami) filed a **federal civil-rights lawsuit** challenging the City of Miami’s **sweeps of homeless encampments**【40†L191-L200】.

The City fought the case but *lost a key motion* in late 2022 when a federal judge denied Miami’s request to dismiss the suit【29†L37-L43】, allowing the challenge to proceed toward trial.

Facing the likelihood of an adverse judgment, the City reached a **settlement in January 2024**, days before trial【40†L191-L199】.

This outcome was hailed by advocates as protecting the due-process rights of homeless residents, though it also underscores that aggressive enforcement policies had crossed legal lines. *(Notably, the Homeless Trust itself was not a defendant in this case – it targeted city practices – but the Trust’s policy stance was aligned with the City.

Ron Book had supported ending Pottinger and advocated for stricter encampment rules, so these civil-rights disputes are closely tied to Trust-supported initiatives.)* **Florida’s Anti-Camping Law (2022–2023):** Beyond local lawsuits, the Homeless Trust has been embroiled in debate over a **new Florida state law (House Bill 1365, 2022)** that many advocates decry as **criminalizing homelessness**.

The law, which fully took effect in 2023–24, makes it illegal for people to **camp or sleep in public** and, starting Jan 1, 2024, even allows *private citizens to sue local governments* that “permit” homeless encampments for damages【27†L71-L78】【39†L275-L283】.

Ron Book **spoke in support of this law** during legislative hearings, arguing it would *“minimize [homeless] encampments”* and force action to get people off the streets【27†L65-L73】.

However, the law’s approach is controversial – it effectively threatens municipalities with lawsuits or state intervention if they don’t remove unsheltered people to sanctioned locations【27†L71-L78】【39†L275-L283】.

This put the Homeless Trust under immense pressure to increase shelter and housing capacity quickly. *“I worry about how fast we can move,”* Book admitted as the deadlines loomed, noting that non-compliance could lead to courts penalizing the county or mandating grim “camp” facilities【27†L73-L81】【27†L93-L99】.

Miami-Dade’s strategy, instead of outdoor camps, has been to fast-track projects like the Cutler Bay motel conversion and other housing for roughly 1,000 people【27†L83-L91】.

Still, civil-rights groups and some local leaders criticize the Trust for **endorsing punitive measures**.

They argue that laws banning public sleeping – and the Trust’s coordination with police on street sweeps – amount to **criminalizing poverty**, which could violate homeless individuals’ rights.

This tension between the Trust’s policies and civil-liberties concerns continues to spark public debate (and likely further legal scrutiny) about how to balance enforcement with compassion. ## CARF Accreditation and Complaints The Miami-Dade Homeless Trust does not directly operate treatment facilities, but it funds and oversees a network of providers (e.g. shelters, rehabilitation programs).

For example, New Hope C.O.R.P.S., a substance abuse treatment provider on the Homeless Trust board, is CARF-accredited【32†L1-L9】【32†L3-L5】. **No public records in the past five years show any CARF violations or sanctions involving the Homeless Trust or its programs.** CARF-related issues typically involve internal service quality standards, and if any Trust-funded program had lost accreditation or been cited, it has not been reported in the media or county oversight reports reviewed.

Book has led the Trust for over **20 years** (since 2004) despite county rules that limit board members to two consecutive 3-year terms and require county residency【17†L85-L94】.

In fact, Book lives in Broward County, but the Miami-Dade Commission has repeatedly **waived its residency and term-limit requirements** to reappoint him, recognizing his influence and track record【17†L85-L93】【17†L89-L97】.

This sentiment indicates a **growing concern about governance** – essentially, an acknowledgment that the Trust’s reliance on one figure isn’t sustainable or fully in line with ethical norms. (It doesn’t appear there have been formal ethics sanctions; a 2021 ethics inquiry cleared a potential conflict-of-interest for a Trust staff member on a grant committee【10†L37-L46】【10†L15-L23】.

However, **public perception issues** remain, as detailed below.) **“Punitive Approach” Complaints:** Perhaps the loudest critiques have come from civil rights and homeless advocacy groups in the community.

They argue the Homeless Trust under Book’s leadership has taken a **punitive, law-enforcement-heavy approach** to homelessness.

MCARE blasted the Trust’s policies as **“unilateral” and aligned more with Book’s private clients’ interests than with the needs of the homeless【19†L156-L164】. (Book is a lobbyist whose clients have included the for-profit prison operator GEO Group, which stands to gain from jailing ordinance violators【19†L173-L181】.) Advocates point to Book’s support for the new state anti-camping law as evidence of a *“punitive approach”* that **criminalizes homelessness**, rather than addressing root causes【19†L163-L172】. *“Cruelty and punitive approaches are not solutions to the trauma of homelessness,”* MCARE’s director David Peery said, calling for **“transparent, trauma-informed leadership”** instead of criminalization【19†L169-L172】.

Likewise, activist Martha Bueno argued that Book’s dual role as homeless chairman and lobbyist for GEO Group is a troubling conflict of interest, accusing him of **“criminaliz[ing] homelessness”** to benefit a private jail company【19†L173-L181】.

These are serious accusations, albeit made in the court of public opinion; they have not (so far) resulted in legal action against the Trust, but they reflect a significant **community trust deficit** with current leadership. **Media and Watchdog Reports:** Local media have kept a close eye on the Homeless Trust’s performance.

Investigative outlets like **Florida Bulldog** and others have highlighted the **paradoxical effects of state policy**, noting the irony that a *“far-right state government [accused of] criminalizing homelessness”* has actually spurred Miami-Dade to invest in more housing (to avoid lawsuits)【39†L239-L247】【39†L242-L250】.

While Book himself has cautioned *“you can’t arrest your way out of homelessness”*【39†L259-L267】, the data suggest enforcement escalated under the new law, raising questions about the Trust’s ability to influence a more humanitarian response.

Overall, the media and watchdog narrative portrays a Homeless Trust that is **aggressively pursuing solutions (housing, outreach)** but is also **mired in controversy** – balancing between county officials pushing for results, a state imposing hardline mandates, and advocates demanding a more compassionate, rights-oriented approach. ## Conclusion **Summary:** In the past five years, the Miami-Dade Homeless Trust has not been implicated in any major financial fraud or malfeasance – audits show generally sound management, aside from minor compliance issues【2†L93-L101】.

However, it faces **persistent scrutiny and complaints** on other fronts: Some observers question its governance (with the same chair exceeding term limits by decades【17†L85-L94】) and approach to homelessness.

Civil-rights advocates have filed lawsuits and won policy changes to stop the **destruction of homeless people’s property** and to check the more punitive aspects of local homelessness policy【40†L191-L200】【40†L205-L214】.

While no **CARF accreditation problems** have been reported, community watchdogs have raised concerns about how the Trust’s strategies intersect with human rights and equity.

In short, the Miami-Dade Homeless Trust is **widely recognized for its achievements in reducing homelessness**, but it has also become a flashpoint for debate – challenged by **auditors, courts, and constituents** to ensure its programs are managed transparently, lawfully, and with respect for the dignity and rights of the homeless population.

The next few years (especially with succession planning underway) may determine how the Trust adapts to these criticisms and legal mandates while continuing its mission to serve one of South Florida’s most vulnerable communities. **Sources:** Recent audits, news articles, and public records were reviewed to compile this overview, including the HUD Inspector General’s audit report【2†L93-L101】, local news coverage by *NBC 6 Miami*【20†L51-L59】【45†L78-L85】, *Miami Herald*/Florida Bulldog reporting【39†L229-L237】【39†L259-L267】, Florida Commission on Ethics inquiries【10†L37-L45】, advocacy group statements (ACLU, Southern Legal Counsel)【40†L191-L200】【40†L205-L213】, and county board documents【17†L85-L94】【19†L156-L164】.

These standards encompass various domains, including leadership, ethical practices, and service delivery. **Relevant CARF Standards:** 1. **Leadership and Ethical Practices:** - Organizations must demonstrate ethical leadership, avoiding conflicts of interest and ensuring decisions prioritize the well-being of those served. - Leaders are expected to uphold integrity and transparency in all organizational operations. 2. **Rights of Persons Served:** - Organizations must protect the rights of individuals, ensuring they are treated with dignity and respect. - Confidentiality and privacy must be maintained, adhering to all relevant laws and regulations. 3. **Service Delivery:** - Services should be person-centered, promoting the health, safety, and well-being of individuals. - Organizations are required to have mechanisms to address grievances and complaints effectively. **Potential Violations by New Hope C.O.R.P.S.:** - **Conflict of Interest:** - Stephen Alvarez serves as both the Executive Director of New Hope C.O.R.P.S. and a Provider Representative on the Board of the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust.

This dual role may lead to decisions that benefit New Hope C.O.R.P.S., potentially compromising ethical leadership standards. - **Rights Violations:** - Instances where New Hope staff allegedly collaborated with law enforcement to evict individuals without proper legal procedures may infringe upon the rights of those served, conflicting with CARF's standards on protecting individual rights. - **Confidentiality Breaches:** - Reports of staff members inquiring about personal health information without consent could constitute breaches of confidentiality, violating CARF's standards on privacy and ethical conduct. **Potential Violations by the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust:** - **Financial Oversight:** - A 2017 audit by the U.S.

Department of Housing and Urban Development's Office of Inspector General found that the Homeless Trust did not always properly administer its Continuum of Care Program, resulting in unsupported costs totaling $98,433.

This lack of oversight may indicate systemic issues in financial management. citeturn0search11 - **Conflict of Interest:** - The presence of individuals in leadership positions who have dual roles in service provider organizations and funding bodies can lead to perceived or actual conflicts of interest, undermining ethical governance. **Conclusion:** Both New Hope C.O.R.P.S. and the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust have potential violations concerning CARF accreditation standards, particularly in areas of ethical leadership, protection of individual rights, and financial oversight.

These issues warrant thorough investigation to ensure compliance with CARF's rigorous standards and to uphold the integrity of services provided to vulnerable populations. > **User (2025-03-07 18:07:07 UTC):** > > Please make canon and add into letter **Assistant (2025-03-07 18:07:08 UTC):** **Complaint Against New Hope C.O.R.P.S. – Submitted to CARF** ### To CARF International, **Why We Are Bringing This Complaint Directly to CARF Instead of the Homeless Trust** We are submitting this complaint directly to CARF due to significant conflicts of interest and ethical concerns within the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust, which undermine our confidence in their ability to handle this matter impartially. **1.

Definition and Scope of Unlawful Detainer Under **Florida Statutes, Chapter 82**, an unlawful detainer action applies when an individual remains on a property **without the legal right to occupy it**, and **no landlord-tenant relationship exists**.

The process must go through **County Court**, and, if granted, results in a **Writ of Possession**—a court order authorizing removal. ### 3.

Role of Law Enforcement Police officers **do not have legal authority** to remove an individual from private property based solely on a verbal request by the owner or other party. **Only the County Sheriff** is authorized to enforce a **Writ of Possession**, after it is issued by a judge.

Any removal effort by law enforcement **prior to judicial process** constitutes **an unlawful eviction** and may expose the officers and municipality to legal liability. ### 4.

Applicable Statute **Florida Statute § 82.03** requires that removal of an unauthorized occupant be handled exclusively through court proceedings.

The statute prohibits **“self-help” evictions**, including threats, coercion, or removal without legal authority. ### 5.

Legal Ramifications If law enforcement removes an occupant without a valid court order, they may be liable for violating **civil rights under federal law**, specifically **42 U.S.C. § 1983**, which protects individuals from government actions carried out without due process. ### Conclusion Unless a **Writ of Possession** has been properly issued and served by the Sheriff’s Office, there is no lawful basis for removing an individual from private property in these circumstances.
